- 341 (2026-10-25, O69's /spec, `libraries: check`, the facet counts of a project board): O69 counts each facet's values over the projects the other filters leave. itemsjs 2.4.4 (Apache-2.0, maintainer cigolpl, last publish 2025-11-25, no runtime dependency, 1.7 MB unpacked) is a faceted search over a list of items that gives each facet's counts under the filters in force, and could replace the counting of `src/lib/state/boards/board/facets.ts` (`countValues`) and the facet loop of `applyFilters` in `state.ts`. (a) write the counting by hand over `facetValues`: each facet counted over the items that every other facet's values, the search, stale, deadline and Mine only leave. Pros: one filter path, the `applyFilters` the board already draws by, with the words and orders of `facets.ts` kept; no dependency. Cons: a few lines written by hand. (b) itemsjs. Pros: a maintained faceted engine. Cons: a second filter path beside `applyFilters`, its own full-text search beside the board's `haystack`, each item's facet values copied into its index, and a package of 1.7 MB for a few lines. Recommended (a); neither option leaves the work undone, so neither is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-25 in automode with (a) (Z994, `plan.md` Rulings, `choices.md`).
- 339 (2026-10-25, O60's look at landing, the Settings view): O60 makes the catalogs and the interface words settable rows of Settings, and a row's editor drew every entry of its value once unfolded: the Text row holds 6053 entries and the `latex_unimath` row 2415, and a Settings search whose word is found inside a row's entries unfolds the row (`isFolded`, `src/lib/state/system/config/view/fold.ts`). With the search `stages` standing, Settings answered after 6268 ms and held 2,211,677 page nodes, against 41,520 on main before O60 (the look's DevTools probe over the scratch servers). (a) a list of entries draws its first 100 and a line Show 100 more of n entries for the next 100, and Add draws through to the entry it adds. Pros: every entry stays editable in Settings, and no search or unfold draws more than 100 cards at a press. Cons: an entry far down a catalog takes several presses, and a search shows a row's first entries rather than those it is found in. (b) a row past a count draws no card editor, only its count and Open Settings File, as VS Code's Edit in settings.json does. Pros: the least code. Cons: the catalogs O60 makes settable cannot be edited in Settings. (c) a virtual list that draws only the cards on screen. Pros: no press. Cons: cards of varying height with nested lists, the largest code. (d) leave it. Cons: Settings freezes on almost any search. Recommended (a); (d) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-25 in automode with (a) (Z993, `plan.md` Rulings, `choices.md`); built at O60's landing.
- 338 (2026-10-25, O60's review over repair round 1, findings S1, B2 and B4 of `agents/reviews/O60-refuter.md`; ruling Z991): Z991 rules what a hub's `<word>: null` does to oculus's own word lists, and leaves three cases its text does not settle. First, a word the hub takes out by replacing a list whole (`roles: [ { key: lead, ... } ]`) still sits in oculus's own values that name it, and the readers give findings on oculus's `config/defaults/layout.yml` (`boards.supervision.role`, `boards.coauthor.role`), the class Z991 rejected. Second, a one-word field of oculus's own values that names a removed word and cannot be left empty (a board's `role`, which takes one role) has no form Z991 gives. Third, a list the hub wrote itself and that names a word the hub removed gives a finding only for `categories`, from that key's reader, at the merged list's index (`categories[2]`) where the hub's file holds the row as `categories[0]`; a hub's own `focus_lanes`, `fund_stages` and `audit` rows give none. (a) the hub layer settles all three in one rule: a word a hub's value no longer gives, whether taken out with `<word>: null` or by a list replaced whole, leaves every word list of oculus's own values that names it, as Z991 says; a one-word field of oculus's own values that names it keeps the word and gets one finding naming the hub's file and the hub's key that took the word out; and a list or row the hub wrote itself that names such a word gets one finding per word naming the hub's file and the word's place in that file, the word then left out of the merged value so no reader words it a second time. The option defaults Settings compares against take the same removal, so no modified bar is drawn on a row nobody set. Pros: every finding a hub's removal gives names the hub's own file at the place the reader edits, with no duplicate and nothing silent. Cons: the merged value of a hub-written list differs from the file by the dead word. (b) Z991's text as it stands: `<word>: null` only, the hub's own lists left as written and worded by their readers. Cons: a hub that replaces `roles` gets findings on oculus's file; its own lanes and funding rows go silent. Recommended (a); (b) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-25 in automode with (a) (Z992, `plan.md` Rulings, `choices.md`); fixed at O60's landing. Correction at O60's landing: no field of oculus's own values holds one such word, so the one-word clause applies to nothing: a board's `tier` and `role` are `control: multi` word lists (`config/options/layout.yml:131`, `:135`) and lose the word with no finding, and a board left with `role: []` or `tier: []` takes no project (`src/lib/data/shapes/boards/takes.ts:133-137`); the one `control: choice` field fed by such a list, the key of `new_project_dir` (`config/options/sources.yml:31`), is the hub's own (`config/defaults/sources.yml:21` ships `{}`).

(a) the merge also takes a word the hub removed out of each word list of oculus's own values that names words of that key (a value the hub did not set itself), so the removal reads with no finding, the Settings edit is accepted, and case 2 holds as written; a list the hub set itself is left as written, and a word of it the hub removed stays a finding naming the hub's file; a row left with an empty word list stays. Pros: one removal does what it says, in the file and in Settings alike. Cons: oculus's rows change without the hub naming them. (b) such a removal is refused at load as the Settings edit refuses it: a finding naming the hub's file and `stages.nascent`, and oculus's word stands. Pros: file and Settings agree. Cons: case 2's value no longer removes `nascent` unless the hub also replaces every row naming it. (c) leave it. Cons: a finding the reader cannot act on in the file it names, and file and Settings disagree. Recommended (a); (c) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-25 in automode with (a) (Z991, `plan.md` Rulings, `choices.md`).

- 328 (2026-10-24, Axel on the project card; read from `src/lib/views/shared/card/project/ProjectCard.svelte:380-399`, `CardLinks.svelte`, `src/lib/views/shared/card/card.ts:146-151`): the card has two kinds of row. The lines under it (Funding, Pillar, Datasets, Team, Related, `CardLinks.svelte`) each hold a list of names, so each line starts with its kind word. (b) the lane icons, and a kind word drawn before each fact of the top row ("Lane Review", "Code deposited", "Venue ..."). Pros: readable without hovering. Cons: a longer row that wraps sooner on a narrow card. (c) the lane icons alone. Cons: "deposited" and the venue still do not say what they are. Recommended (a); (c) is the lazy option. Cons: changes the key name ruling 297 gave. (b) `settings: { language: <id> }` as ruling 297 says. Cons: one word for two things in the same settings. Recommended (a); (b) is the lazy option. (a) ruling 299's rule by shape for every one of them: a map merges by word, a word the hub names replacing oculus's row in oculus's place, a new word after oculus's, `<word>: null` removing one; a list is replaced whole, since its order is its meaning (`publication_kinds` is read first row first, `venue_tiers` is a ranking). A key oculus ships no value for merges with nothing, so the hub's value reads as written. Pros: one rule for each shape, the one Axel gave. Cons: none found. (b) every one of them replaced whole, as today. Pros: no change for these keys. Cons: a hub that changes one colour of `palette` copies all 40 rows. Recommended (a); (b) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24 in automode with (a) (Z978, `plan.md` Rulings, `choices.md`).
- 310 (2026-10-24, O60's /spec, the booking of 2026-10-23 under O60 that each hub's records take their own hub's settings, with no primary as with one): once every list of `vocab.yml` is a hub key, two hubs of one workspace may hold different words, and a view that draws a list over the records of several hubs (a board's stage buttons, its Role and Category facets, the Focus lanes) draws one list. (a) a record is read under its own hub's values (the label and colour of its stage, its form and chips, its category, its lane, its status), and a list drawn over the records of several hubs holds the words of every hub of the workspace, the primary first and then the others in the workspace's order, each word once with the row of the first hub that holds it; a form or a control for one record offers its own hub's list. Pros: every record shows its own hub's words; no word goes unknown on a shared view. Cons: a word two hubs colour differently shows one colour in a shared facet. (b) the primary's lists alone (oculus's with no primary), a record's word outside them drawn as an unknown word. Pros: one list, no union. Cons: a hub's own words show as unknown on every shared view, which the booking asks O60 to end. Recommended (a); (b) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24 in automode with (a) (Z977, `plan.md` Rulings, `choices.md`).
- 308 (2026-10-24, O55, kind 3 of self-rule: a script the brief does not build). The review over O55's round 1 (`agents/reviews/O55-refuter.md`, "Repair round 1, refuted", finding 2) found that the builder wrote three Python programs in the session scratchpad (`scratchpad/o55/`) and used them to make edits, which O55's brief forbids without your approval ("No script is written for this step ... other than a script the step builds"). They compute no judgment: `presets_edit.py` replaces fixed blocks of `src/lib/state/boards/board/presets.ts` with new text written into the program (types, imports, `presetFor`, `boardIds`), each replacement asserting its old text is there; `rewrap.py` rewraps one comment paragraph at 100 columns; `r1-assemble.py` rewrites the builder's own report from text files it wrote. Every line they produced is in the diff, which I read in full and two reviewers read. The skill keeps their output out of main until you rule, so O55's landing, and O60, O63, O66 and O68 behind it, wait on this. (a) Accept the three programs for O55 after the fact, as edit helpers whose whole output was read in the diff, and land O55 as built with the fixes at landing. Pro: the result is the text the builder would have written with the editor; nothing waits. Con: a brief's script rule gets an exception after the fact. (b) Redo by hand what they produced: the `presets.ts` changes and the rewrapped comment paragraphs, by the session at landing with the editor, and the report's edits likewise. Pro: the rule is kept to the letter. Con: the result is the same text, byte for byte, at the cost of the rework; the rules allow no second repair round, so the session does it inline. (c) Take O55 back and build it again from its brief. Con: a full rebuild for the same text. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: the rule exists so that no unread computation decides content, and here every output line is in a diff that was read whole. (b) is not the lazy option, since it does the same work twice; the lazy option is landing O55 without asking, which this item exists to prevent. Ruled (a) by Axel 2026-10-24 (`plan.md` Rulings, Open item 308).
- 307 (2026-10-24, O55, rulings 304 and 306; Axel asked how the rail decides what it counts): a board gives the rail one number, `boardPart` (`src/lib/state/workbench/rail-badges/rail-badges.svelte.ts:164-169`): the count of the board's first group in that group's colour, or the board's cards when it has no groups; the rail button adds up the numbers of the views on its page whose row of `counts` says `rail: true` (`src/lib/shell/rail/RailPages.svelte:1-8`, `src/lib/state/workbench/navigation/registry.ts:228-232`), which on the boards are projects, coauthor and personal, archive and software saying `rail: false` (`config/defaults/layout.yml:302-306`). The first group is the cards in work only because each board's `groups:` lists in progress first (`layout.yml:238-298`). Under ruling 304 (1) (a) and (2) (a) a board's first category is the first row of `categories:` that holds one of its projects, which changes with the data: on the projects board, under 306 (1) (a), it is `in-review` while any project is submitted, else `in-flight`, else `idea`. (a) a row of `categories:` may carry `rail: true`, and a board gives the rail the count of its projects in those rows, in the colour of the first of them; shipped on `in-flight` alone. Pro: one place decides it for every board, and the number never depends on which rows happen to hold projects. The rail then counts the work to be done; a submitted paper, whose lane Review is `parked` ("hands off"), is not counted. Con: a row of the vocabulary names a surface of the shell. (b) the same flag, shipped on `in-flight` and `in-review`. Pro: the rail counts what it counts today, every live project. Con: a paper waiting on a journal raises the badge though nothing is to be done on it. (c) each board's row of `counts` names the categories it gives the rail (`projects: { tab: true, rail: true, categories: [in-flight] }`). Con: each board chooses again, the per-board choice ruling 303 ended. (d) a board gives the rail its cards. Con: the rail counts ideas and finished work, and the coauthor board's published papers raise it for good. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: the rail badge asks for attention, and a submitted paper asks for none. (d) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 306 (2026-10-24, O55 and O68, rulings 303, 304 and 305; Axel: putting `revision` in the in-review row is a bug): the stage `revision` is "major revision (back with the authors)" (`config/catalogs/vocab.yml:74`), and its Focus lane Revise is in the cluster `now`, "back from a venue (R&R or reject)" (`vocab.yml:177`), while `submitted`'s lane Review is in the cluster `parked`, "under peer review, hands off" (`vocab.yml:178`): a project at `revision` is work on the authors' desk, not work waiting on a venue. (1) The split of in progress in `categories:`. (a) two rows, in this order: `in-review` (label "in review", `stages: { words: [submitted] }`, `record: true`), then `in-flight` (label "in flight", `stages: { flags: [live] }`), so a submitted project is in review and every other live stage (de-risking, exploring, in-prep, building, submit-ready, revision) is in flight; the track record gets a line "in review" per role. `in-review` sets no colour and takes `submitted`'s `#0ea5e9` (ruling 296), which the outputs row also set, so `other-outputs` takes the unused `#9333ea`. Pro: work waiting on a venue is told apart from work being done, on every board and on the record, where a funder asks for submitted work separately. Con: one more row on the boards that hold such projects. (b) the same split with `in-review` not on record. Con: submitted work does not reach the track record. (c) one in-progress row as now. Con: a board cannot tell waiting from working. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. (2) The website row. Ruling 300 (1) moves the rows of `publication_kinds` into `audit: website: kinds` as they are, and its row `{ kind: [papers-in-review], stages: [submitted, revision] }` (`vocab.yml:223`) also puts `revision` with `submitted`. (a) `revision` moves to the preparation row: `{ kind: [papers-in-review], stages: [submitted] }` and `{ kind: [papers-in-preparation], stages: [in-prep, submit-ready, revision] }`. Pro: the website check and the boards place a project at `revision` the same way. Con: a paper back from a named journal with a revision request is listed on the website with the papers not yet submitted. (b) the row kept as it is. Pro: a website reader is told the paper is still with its journal, which is what a publication list's "in review" heading tells that reader. Con: the board and the website file the stage differently. Recommendation: (b), a judgment: the website heading answers a reader of a publication list, the board row answers what the authors have to do. (b) is also the cheaper one, which is not the reason for it. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (1) (a), with `in-flight` also on record (`record: true`), so the track record has an "in flight" and an "in review" line per role; (2) (b); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 305 (2026-10-24, O55 and O68, rulings 303 and 304; Axel: better names than papers and outputs, and in progress split into in flight and in review, which can be on record): the shipped rows of `categories:` would be in-progress (`stages: { flags: [live] }`, de-risking to revision), idea, papers (published journal and conference), outputs (published thesis, abstract, poster, tech-report), released and dead-ends. (1) The names of the two record rows. (a) `publications` (label "publications": journal articles and conference papers) and `other-outputs` (label "other outputs": theses, extended abstracts, posters, tech reports), the two headings a research CV commonly uses. Pro: each name says what it holds, and "other" says it is the lesser list. Con: an abstract is also published, so "publications" reads wider than it holds. (b) `peer-reviewed` (label "peer-reviewed papers") and `other-outputs`. Con: the form does not guarantee peer review (a conference abstract may be reviewed, a thesis is examined), so the name makes a claim the data does not carry. (c) `papers` and `outputs` as now. Con: a paper is an output too, so `outputs` does not say what sets it apart. Recommendation: (a), a judgment. (2) The split of in progress. (a) two rows, in this order: `in-review` (label "in review", `stages: { words: [submitted, revision] }`, `record: true`), then `in-flight` (label "in flight", `stages: { flags: [live] }`), so a submitted or revised project is in review (the first row that holds it) and every other live stage (de-risking, exploring, in-prep, building, submit-ready) is in flight; the track record gets a line "in review" per role. `in-review` sets no colour and takes submitted's `#0ea5e9` (ruling 296), which `outputs` also sets, so `other-outputs` takes the unused `#9333ea`. Pro: work waiting on a journal is told apart from work still being done, on every board and on the record, where a funder asks for submitted work separately. Con: one more row on the boards that hold such projects. (b) the same split with `in-review` not on record. Con: submitted work does not reach the track record. (c) one row as now. Con: a board cannot tell waiting from working. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (1) (a); booked in plan.md Rulings. (2) not ruled: its (a) put `revision` in the in-review row, which Axel named a bug, and it is raised again corrected as open item 306.
- 304 (2026-10-24, O55, ruling 303; Axel asked whether a board's tab lists every category present on the board, and whether every board has the list's order): ruling 303 (1) (a) defines each category once in `categories:` and lets a board's groups refer to rows by key, which leaves three things open. (1) Which categories a board shows. (a) a board names none: it shows every row of `categories:` that holds at least one of its projects, and its tab counts each of them; the board's `groups:` key is removed from `config/defaults/layout.yml`. Pro: no board decides anything about categories, and nothing a board writes can disagree with the list. Con: a board cannot hide a category it holds. On the shipped boards: the personal board shows in progress, idea and released, its building, de-risking and exploring projects in in progress (today it has its own building group and an idea group up to exploring); the archive board's outputs take theses, as the coauthor board's then do too. (b) a board names the rows it shows, `groups: [papers, outputs, dead-ends]`, a key no row of `categories:` has being a finding (a key mistyped, such as `paper`), and a project in a row the board does not name in no group. Pro: a board can leave a category out. Con: each board again chooses, the per-board choice Axel named wrong. Recommendation: (a). (2) The order. (a) every board draws its categories in the order of `categories:`, the order that also decides which row holds a project, so every board has the same order. (b) a board orders the rows it names. Con: only possible under (1) (b), and boards differ again. Recommendation: (a). (3) How a hub changes the list, under ruling 299. (a) by key, as the other map lists of `vocab.yml` (`stages`, `forms`, `chips`): a row the hub names replaces oculus's row in its place, a new row is added after oculus's rows, `<key>: null` removes one. Con: a new row always comes last, so a hub cannot put a new row before an oculus row that would also hold the same projects. (b) whole, as `focus_lanes` and the other row lists, since the order decides which row holds a project. Pro: a hub sets any order. Con: a hub that changes one row writes them all, and misses oculus's later rows. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: `stages:` has the same order effect (ruling 296) and merges by key. None of (1) to (3) has a lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (1) (a), (2) (a), (3) (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 303 (2026-10-24, O55 and O68, rulings 288 (1), 291 and 301; Axel: the track record must tell a paper from an output, and each board deciding what a paper or an output is, is wrong): today each board of `boards:` in `config/defaults/layout.yml:238-298` writes its own groups, so one word has several definitions: `outputs` is abstract, poster and tech-report on the coauthor board (`:255`) and thesis, abstract, poster and tech-report on the archive board (`:270`); `idea` is nascent and scoping on the projects board (`:240`) and nascent to exploring on the personal board (`:286`); `in progress`, `papers` and `dead-ends` are written twice with the same words. Ruling 291 keeps the groups per board. The track record of ruling 301 needs one meaning of paper and of output, and today there is none to read. Other readers classify on their own: the Pillars view's Archival band reads the stage flag `dead` (`src/lib/state/boards/structure/lines.ts:103-112`), and the website check reads its own `kinds` (ruling 300). (1) Where a category is defined. (a) once, in one list of `config/catalogs/vocab.yml`: each row a key, a label, a colour, and the `stages:` and `forms:` blocks of ruling 288 (1) that say which projects it holds, a project taking the first row in the list's order that holds it (ruling 291's rule, moved from the board to the list); a board's `groups:` becomes a list of those keys, for example the archive board `groups: [papers, outputs, dead-ends]`; a key no row has is a finding; the Pillars view's Archival band reads the dead-ends row. Pro: one meaning of each word, read by every board, the track record and the Pillars view; a hub changes a category once (ruling 299 merges the list by key). Con: a board can no longer give one word its own meaning; the personal board's `idea` (nascent to exploring) and the projects board's (nascent, scoping) become one row, so the personal board's de-risking and exploring projects move to the row that holds them, `in progress`. (b) a form carries what it is, `forms: { journal: { kind: paper } }`, and the stage says the rest; the groups stay per board. Pro: smaller change. Con: the boards still define `in progress`, `idea` and `dead-ends` each on their own, which is the fault named. (c) as now, per board. Con: the fault named stays, and the track record has nothing to count. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. (2) The name of the list, a vocabulary word every hub reads: (a) `categories:`, the word the board's filter already uses for this facet (`bucketOf`, `src/lib/state/boards/board/presets.ts:360-367`); (b) `outcomes:`. Con of (b): `in progress` and `idea` are not outcomes. Recommendation: (a). (3) Which categories the track record counts: (a) each row carrying the flag `record: true` (shipped: papers and outputs), each counted on its own line per role, never summed into one number, so a paper and an output are never the same value; (b) every category. Con of (b): it counts dead-ends and work in progress as record. Recommendation: (a). Neither of (2) and (3) has a lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (1) (a), (2) (a), (3) (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 302 (2026-10-24, O68, rulings 292 and 294; Axel: after ruling 301 the summary card seems useless): the summary card of rulings 292 and 294 lists each board with the count of each of its groups. Those counts are drawn already: each board's tab draws the count of each of its groups in the group's colour, with the words on hover, and the view catalogue draws the same badges (`boardCells`, `src/lib/state/workbench/rail-badges/rail-badges.svelte.ts:1-2,304-312`; `src/lib/views/boards/projects/Projects.svelte:3-6,17`), and the rail sums the first group of the boards whose `counts` row says `rail: true` (`config/defaults/layout.yml:302-307`). Ruling 301 puts on the career card the counts about one person, by role. (a) no summary card: rulings 292 and 294 are replaced, and O68 builds the lane card marks and the career card only. Pro: no number is drawn in two places, and Focus keeps the parts nothing else shows. Con: Focus has no one place listing every board's groups; that list is the view catalogue's badges. (b) the summary card as ruled. Pro: every board's counts on the board you open first. Con: it repeats the tabs' and the catalogue's numbers, and with the track record a third count of published work. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: the card would add no number the workbench does not already draw. (a) costs less to build, but it is not the lazy option, since it leaves nothing of what Focus needs undone. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 301 (2026-10-24, O68, amends ruling 293; Axel: what else would be useful on the career card beside the situation?): the career card is the one part of Focus about one person's career, where they stand and where they are going. The rest of Focus already shows: dated work and deadlines of projects, funding schemes, dated collection entries, leads and follow-ups in the Command Center (`src/lib/state/boards/ticker.ts:188-441`); undated tasks in Tasks; the projects by stage in the lanes; and, after O68, the counts of each board's groups in the summary card (ruling 294). What none of them shows, and the career card could: (1) the goal: one line naming the position or step aimed at; (2) the next milestone: the next career step with its date; (3) the order of work: the few priorities, in order, that decide what to do first; (4) the track record: the counts a funder or a hiring committee reads, computed from the projects and their people fields (ruling 288 (2)): published work with the workspace's `self` as `lead`, as `supervisors`, and as `collaborators`, and funding obtained with `self` as `lead`; drawn only when `self` is set. (4) is not the summary card of ruling 294: the summary card counts every project in each board's groups, whoever's work it is (ruling 292), while (4) counts only the work that names `self`, split by the role `self` holds, and the funding `self` won as lead, which no board group counts. In a hub with one person the published counts of the two can be close; the role split and the funding are what (4) adds. (a) the situation, (1), (2), (3) and (4). Pro: the card says where one person stands, where they are going, what comes first and what they can show, and nothing of it repeats another Focus card. Con: (4) is new code, a count over the projects. (b) the situation, (1), (2) and (3), without the track record. Pro: no new count. Con: the counts that applications ask for are not on the board. (c) the situation only, as ruling 293 (a) reads. Con: the card then holds facts with no direction and nothing to act on. Either way the host pipeline, the host criteria, the eligibility rules, the dropped list and `scoreTier`'s fixed words go, as ruling 293 says. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: (2) and (3) are the parts a reader acts on and (4) is the standing a career plan is judged by. (c) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 300 (2026-10-24, O63, ruling 220, Axel: make the website audit general for oculus; Axel asked what `standard`, `outcome_folders`, the move of `publication_kinds` and the finding texts mean): the hub conventions are fixed in code today (`src/lib/server/content/audit/projects.ts`): the README the stages flagged `standard` must have (`:311-323`), the retired documents `RETIRED` (`:51`), the folder renames `FOLDERS` (`:54-58`), `code/README.md` when `code/` exists (`:345-353`), the README's `## Data` section when `datasets` is set (`:136-140`), the outcome folders (`folderStage`, `:296-304`), and the website feed: the file `publications_file`, read as `<section>: { papers: [ { title } ] }` (`audit/publications.ts:87-103`), its sections from `publication_kinds` in `config/catalogs/vocab.yml`. A `find` of the hub on 2026-10-24 found none of the retired files and no folder named `paper`, `experiment` or `pipeline` in a project. The flag `standard` sits on exactly the stages without `terminal` (nascent to revision) and its one reader is the README check (`projects.ts:314`). The flag `archive_outcome` (published, failed, stopped) has one reader, `folderStage`. (1) The shape. (a) one hub key `audit:` on the hub list, each convention off unless set: `audit: { required_files: [ { file: README.md, stages: { not_flags: [terminal] } }, { file: code/README.md, folder: code } ], required_sections: [ { file: README.md, heading: '## Data', when: datasets } ], website: { file: side-projects/theaxec.github.io/_data/publications.yml, list: papers, title: title, kinds: [ { kind: [papers-in-review], stages: [submitted, revision] }, ... ] } }`, the rows of today's `publication_kinds` moved as they are, the website file's top-level keys being its sections. `publication_kinds` leaves `vocab.yml` and becomes `audit: website: kinds:`, since it says which section of one hub's website a stage belongs in and no other reader uses it. The flag `standard` is removed, its one reader written as `not_flags: [terminal]`. Pro: every convention is one readable block a hub turns on, and a hub with none gets none. Con: one larger key. (b) one top-level hub key per convention. Con: several keys to find. Recommendation: (a). (2) The retired documents and folder renames: (a) removed from the code with no setting: they find renames from an older layout of this hub, none of which exists now, and a hub that changes its layout rewrites its files once (the rule on migration code). (b) kept as `audit:` keys. Con: a setting whose only use is a layout no hub has. Recommendation: (a). (3) The outcome folders: the check that a project filed under `archive/<outcome>/` is at the stage the folder names, and that a published project is filed under such a folder. (a) removed with the flag `archive_outcome`: the stage is the project's state and no reader of the folder remains. (b) kept as `audit: { outcome_folders: true }`. Con: a filing rule of this hub's layout in oculus. Recommendation: (a). (4) The finding texts (`config/text/views/system/audit.yml:55-58,69`) say this hub's policy in oculus's shipped words: `msg_no_readme` "the one private doc every project needs", `msg_retired_doc` "fold it into README.md", `msg_site_negative` "hub-private negative result ... (null/negative results never leave the hub)". (a) each says what the setting found, in words true for any hub: `msg_no_readme` "missing `{0}`, a file the hub's audit setting requires at this stage", `msg_site_negative` "stage `{0}` is flagged `negative` and appears on the website under {1}"; a hub that wants its own words sets them by the override of open item 297 (2). (b) as now. Con: every hub reads one hub's policy. Recommendation: (a). The lazy option would be leaving the code's lists, which ruling 220 ended. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (1) (a), (2) (a), (3) (a), (4) (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 297 (2) (2026-10-24, O60, ruling 214; part (1) ruled (a), see plan.md Rulings): how a workspace changes single interface words, laid over the chosen language. (a) the workspace file's `settings:` takes `text:` with dotted keys of oculus's catalog (`config/text/`), for example `text: { focus.lanes.idea: Ideas }`; an unknown key is a finding. Pro: one word changed in the file that already holds the workspace's settings, as ruling 214 asks. Con: none beyond the key. (b) a `text/` folder beside the workspace file, holding catalog files. Pro: the same file shape as a language app. Con: a second place to look for one word. (c) no override; every change of a word is a language app. Pro: one mechanism. Con: undoes ruling 214, and one word needs a whole app. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 296 (2026-10-24, O55, rulings 269 and 288; Axel asked why (a)): ruling 269 (b) gives a board group without `color:` the colour of its first word's stage, and ruling 288 (1) with 291 (a) lets a group name its stages by flag ("in flight" `stages: { flags: [live] }`, dead-ends `stages: { flags: [dead] }`) or by form alone, so such a group has no first stage word; forms carry no colour (`config/catalogs/vocab.yml` `forms:`). Every shipped board group sets `color:` (`config/defaults/layout.yml:239-286`), so neither option changes a shipped colour; the question is only a group a hub writes without `color:`. (a) such a group takes the colour of the first stage, in the order of `stages:`, that its `stages:` block takes, and a group with no `stages:` block and no `color:` is a finding. A hub's in-flight group by `flags: [live]` would be de-risking's `#16a34a`, the colour the shipped "in progress" group sets; a hub's dead-ends group by `flags: [dead]` would be failed's red `#dc2626`, where the shipped dead-ends group sets stopped's grey `#9ca3af`. Pro: one rule for word and flag groups, the rule ruling 269 (b) already gives word groups, and no group without a colour. Con: the colour follows the order of `stages:`, so a hub that reorders its stages can change a group's colour without touching the group. (b) a group that names its stages by flag or not at all must set `color:`, its absence a finding. Pro: no colour depends on an order. Con: two rules for one key, and a hub writes a colour for each flag group. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: it extends the rule already ruled for word groups to flag groups, and the order effect is the same one a word group has today when its first word is reordered. Neither is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 294 (2026-10-24, Axel: is the 292 summary its own Focus card or part of the career card?): ruling 292 (a) lists each board of `boards:` with the count of each of its groups; ruling 293 (a) cuts the career card to the `situation` strip of the hub's `career.yml` (`src/lib/views/boards/focus/career/CareerPanel.svelte`). (a) its own Focus card, drawn where the Command Center's cards are, shown whatever the hub's career file holds. Pro: a hub with no career file still gets the summary, and the career card stays the facts about one person, which a hub opened by several people (`self` per workspace) does not share. Con: one more card on Focus. (b) a part of the career card, under the situation strip. Pro: one card for where the user stands. Con: the counts of the hub's boards would sit under one person's facts, and a hub without `career.yml` has no summary. Recommendation: (a). Neither is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 295 (2026-10-24, Axel: which step carries rulings 290, 292 and 293?): no step of `plan.md` carries them: O55 changes the boards and `focus.ts`'s lane test but not `LaneCard.svelte`, the Command Center's cards or the career card, and O60, O63 and O66 are other concerns. Ruling 290 also fixes the lane card's cap of two marks (`CARD_CHIPS = 2`, `src/lib/views/boards/focus/lanes/LaneCard.svelte:78`, set when the Focus board was first built, `git log -S CARD_CHIPS`: a4687984), which hides every mark past the second with no way to see it on the card; it is the only view that cuts the status marks (`projectStatusWords` has two readers, the board list draws every word, `Projects.svelte:231-237`). (a) one new step O68 after O55, the Focus board: a lane card draws every mark its lane does not already say, wrapping onto a second line when they do not fit (ruling 290); the summary card (rulings 292, 294); the career card cut to the situation strip, its host pipeline, host editor, `career_host_status`, `career_score_dims` and the awaiting box's hosts removed, and the hub's career notes moved into `funding/2026-fwo-senior-transplant/` as a diff committed on Axel's word (ruling 293). Pro: one step for one view, after O55 has settled the boards the summary reads. Con: the cap stays until O68 lands. (b) as (a), with the cap removal and ruling 290 folded into O55. Pro: the bug goes with the next landing. Con: O55 widens to a view it does not otherwise touch. (c) two new steps, O68 the lane cards and summary, O69 the career card. Con: two landings over the same Focus files. Recommendation: (a). The lazy option would be a roadmap entry, which leaves the bug in place with no step to end it. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 298 (2026-10-24, O60, ruling 214: a hub's `config.yml` may add fields to the project form): the form is `manifest_schema:` in `config/schemas/manifest-schema.yml` (sections and fields, each with `type`, `from`, `section`, `help`), which oculus also turns into the JSON Schema of every `project.yml`; a hub key replaces oculus's key whole (the hub's `config.yml:3`). (a) a hub key `manifest_fields:`, a list of field rows in the shape of `manifest_schema`'s fields, added after oculus's own, a row naming a field oculus has or a section the form lacks being a finding; each added field is then a known key of `project.yml`. Pro: a hub adds a field without copying the form. Con: a second key beside `manifest_schema`. (b) the hub may set `manifest_schema:` whole. Con: the hub copies every field and misses oculus's later ones. Recommendation: (a). (b) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 299 (2026-10-24, O60, ruling 214: the `vocab.yml` lists join the hub list): today a hub key replaces oculus's value of that key whole, so a hub that adds one stage writes every stage again. (a) a hub's map list (`stages`, `forms`, `chips`, `tiers`, `statuses`, `lead_statuses`) merges with oculus's by word: a word the hub names replaces oculus's row of that word, a new word is added after oculus's, and `<word>: null` removes one; a list of rows (`focus_lanes`, `roles`, `publication_kinds`, `fund_stages`, `priority_levels`) is replaced whole, since its order is its meaning. Pro: a hub writes only what differs. Con: two merge rules, by kind of list. (b) every hub key replaces whole, as today. Con: a hub copies oculus's vocabulary and misses later words. Recommendation: (a). (b) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 293 (2026-10-24, Axel: does most of `career.yml` belong in an md file of `funding/2026-fwo-senior-transplant`?): the hub's `.oculus/data/academic/career.yml` holds `situation` (a fact strip), `order` (one task), `hosts` (one host, 15 commented out, each with a status and four scores), `host_criteria`, `eligibility` (the FWO Senior rule, shots and caveat) and a dropped list; all but `situation` and `order` are the FWO Senior application's own working notes. Oculus builds a career card on it: the host pipeline with `career_host_status` and `career_score_dims` from the hub's `config.yml:70-80`, the host editor (`server/edit/entry-edit/host-status.ts`), `CareerPanel.svelte`, `CareerHosts.svelte`, `state/boards/career.ts`, and the Focus awaiting box's hosts (`state/boards/focus.ts:419-421`); 44 modules name `career`. (a) the hub moves `hosts`, `host_criteria`, `eligibility` and the dropped list into a markdown file in `funding/2026-fwo-senior-transplant/`, `career.yml` keeps `situation` only, and oculus's career card becomes the situation strip: the host pipeline, its editor, `career_host_status`, `career_score_dims` and the hosts of the awaiting box go. Pro: the application's notes sit with the application, and oculus loses a card built for one application. Con: the host statuses no longer show on Focus. (b) the hub moves the notes and oculus keeps the card for any hub that fills it. Con: code no hub uses. (c) as now. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. Carried by: needs a step; options: a new step of this plan after O55, or a roadmap entry; Axel's word schedules it; the hub's move is shown as a diff and committed on Axel's word, as ruling 286 was. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 292 (2026-10-24, Axel: a summary on Focus of papers, outputs, dead-ends, coauthorships, ideas, in progress and in review, per tier): Focus has no such count today; the board statistics strip counts one board ("53 projects | 2 submit-ready | 0 blocked | 1 dated"). After O55 the boards (Projects, Archive, Supervision, Coauthor, Personal, Software) and their groups say each of those words, coauthorship being the Coauthor board. (a) a Focus card that lists each board of `boards:` with the count of each of its groups, read from the boards' own keys, so a hub's boards and groups make its summary and no new vocabulary is added. Pro: one definition of each word. Con: the summary follows the boards, not tiers (Personal and Software are tiers and boards both; Projects, Archive, Supervision and Coauthor split the tier research by role and stage). (b) a summary per tier with a fixed list of counts. Con: a second definition of papers, ideas and the rest. (c) none. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. Carried by: needs a step; options: a new step of this plan after O55, or a roadmap entry; Axel's word schedules it. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a), with where it lives as a new open item; booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 291 (2026-10-24, Axel: how do papers and outputs work?): a board group is one value of the board's category filter, and a project takes the first group, in the board's order, whose words hold its stage, its form or a chip (`bucketOf`, `src/lib/state/boards/board/presets.ts:360-367`, read by `facets.ts:27`); a project no group takes has no category, so the filter cannot pick it. The groups ruled under 288 (1) name forms alone for papers and outputs, so on Coauthor and Archive a `failed` or `stopped` project with `form: journal` is in papers, not dead-ends, and an in-flight journal paper on Coauthor is in flight only because that group comes first. (a) each group names its stage as well: papers `stages: { words: [published] }, forms: { words: [journal, conference] }`, outputs `stages: { words: [published] }, forms: { words: [thesis, abstract, poster, tech-report] }`, dead-ends `stages: { flags: [dead] }`, "in flight" and idea as ruled; a project in no group keeps no category. Pro: a group means the same whatever its place in the list. Con: longer group rows. (b) the groups as ruled under 288 (1), order deciding. Con: a failed journal paper is listed under papers. Recommendation: (a), which changes the shipped groups of ruling 288 (1). (b) is the lazy option. Carried by: O55. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 290 (2026-10-24, Axel: does a lane card need the stage when the lane has one stage, or Backburner and Blocked their chip?): a lane card draws at most two marks (`CARD_CHIPS = 2`, `src/lib/views/boards/focus/lanes/LaneCard.svelte:78`), from the stage when it has a glyph, then the form, then the chips (`projectStatusWords`, `src/lib/state/shared/words/tones.ts:251-258`); so on Run, Scope, De-risk, Build, Review, Revise and the Idea lane the first mark repeats the lane, and on Backburner and Blocked a chip mark repeats it, and either can push a chip that says something new (results-in, admin-prep) off the card. (a) a lane card leaves out a mark the lane already says: the stage when the lane lists that stage alone, the lane's own chip on a chip lane; Push (in-prep, submit-ready) keeps the stage. Pro: the two marks say something the lane does not. Con: a card dragged off its lane changes what it draws. (b) the stage is never drawn on a lane card, the chips are. Con: Push loses in-prep against submit-ready. (c) as now. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. Carried by: a step Axel names (O55 does not touch `LaneCard.svelte`). Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a), and called the two-mark cap a major bug; booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 289 (2026-10-24, Axel: FedBTD is `stopped` but shows on Backburner): a project at a `terminal` stage that carries the chip `parked` or `blocked` sits on Backburner or Blocked, since `laneOf` (`src/lib/state/boards/focus.ts:247-258`) takes the chip lanes first whatever the stage; ruling 272 put FedBTD there on purpose ("FedBTD goes to Backburner by the chip parked (stage stopped, ruling 281 (a))"), and `stopped` means dropped or abandoned while `parked` means deprioritized, not being worked (`config/catalogs/vocab.yml:78,181`). (a) a chip lane holds only a project whose stage is not `terminal`, a `terminal` project carrying `parked` or `blocked` is an audit finding, and the hub's rewrite takes `parked` off FedBTD; replaces the FedBTD part of ruling 272. Pro: a finished project never reads as paused work. Con: one new finding. (b) as (a) without the finding. Con: a stale chip stays unseen. (c) as now. Con: a dropped project shows among work to come back to. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. Carried by: O55 (it rewrites `focus.ts`'s tier test at `holds`, so the lane rule is in its paths) unless Axel names a step. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (a); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 288 (2026-10-23, /spec O55): three shapes O55 needs that no ruling names. (1) How a board and a board group name the state they take. Ruling 213 named a `type:` block and a `status:` block, each holding `words`, `not_words`, `flags` and `not_flags`, and ruling 215 keeps a group's words apart by list. Since then the manifest's type became `stage:` (ruling 252), its form moved to its own field `form:` with the list `forms:` (ruling 253), and the project chips are the list `chips:` (ruling 221); today a group has one `keys` list mixing all three (`config/defaults/layout.yml:226-300`, read by `bucketOf` in `src/lib/state/boards/board/presets.ts`), and the shipped papers and outputs groups name forms. (a) three blocks named after the vocabulary list each reads, `stages:`, `forms:` and `chips:`, as a Focus lane's `stages:` and `chips:` are named (`config/catalogs/vocab.yml:174-186`); `stages:` and `chips:` hold the four lists, `forms:` holds `words` and `not_words` only, since no form carries a flag; boards and groups take the same blocks and a group's `keys` goes. The shipped boards and groups would read: Projects `tier: research, role: lead, stages: { not_flags: [terminal] }`; Archive `tier: research, role: lead, stages: { flags: [terminal] }`; Supervision `tier: research, role: supervisor`; Coauthor `tier: research, role: [collaborator, other]`; Personal `tier: personal`; Software `tier: software`; the group in progress `stages: { words: [de-risking, exploring, in-prep, building, submit-ready, submitted, revision] }`, idea `stages: { words: [nascent, scoping] }`, papers `forms: { words: [journal, conference] }`, outputs `forms: { words: [thesis, abstract, poster, tech-report] }`, dead-ends `stages: { words: [failed, stopped] }`; the group in progress holds exactly the `live` stages, so under (a) it reads `stages: { flags: [live] }` labelled "in flight", the word ruling 275 gives the live cross-link group and the Focus board already draws (`config/text/views/boards/focus.yml:99`); its id comes from the label (`slugOf`, `state/boards/board/presets.ts:293`). Pro: one block per list, every word checked against its own list, the same names as the lanes. Con: three block names to learn. (b) the same three blocks named after the manifest field, `stage:`, `form:` and `chips:`. Pro: the name is the field a reader sees in the manifest. Con: a lane says `stages:` and a board says `stage:` for the same words. (c) the two blocks of ruling 213 only, renamed `stages:` and `chips:`, a form named through `match: { form: [journal, conference] }`, which groups would then also take. Pro: no third block. Con: a form is checked against no list, so a misspelt form is no finding, and groups gain `match:`. Recommendation: (a). (c) is the lazy option. (2) The `roles:` list of ruling 206 (2) and a project or collection entry that names no one. The fields are the manifest's: `lead:` the first author or authors, `supervisors:` the last author, `collaborators:` the other authors who count (Axel, 2026-10-24); `lead:` is in 94 of the hub's 114 manifests, `collaborators:` in 37, `supervisors:` in none. The rows, highest first: `- { key: lead, label: Lead, field: lead }`, `- { key: supervisor, label: Supervisor, field: supervisors }`, `- { key: collaborator, label: Collaborator, field: collaborators }`, `- { key: other, label: Other }`; the user's role is the first row whose field holds the person the workspace setting `self` names (`selfIds`, `state/boards/board/team.ts:18-20`), and `other` when the entry names people but not the user (21 manifests today). Naming no one (Axel 2026-10-24: is that not a finding?): 10 manifests have no `lead:` (3 software, 7 personal); no hub collection declares any of the three fields; the work collections hold their people as free text today: funding's `Role` meta row (`contributor` on ZonMw-AI-HCC, `lead - health use cases` on FAIR, `.oculus/data/academic/funding.yml:77,115`), the education entries' `Role` meta row, the events' own `role:` field. `self` is a workspace setting (`config/defaults/sources.yml:77`, set in the hub's `.oculus/workspaces/<name>.yml`) and a hub's files are shared, so one hub can be opened by several people each naming their own `self`: an entry that names no one belongs to no one in particular, and oculus cannot know that a collection is one person's (Axel 2026-10-24: what if `self` is not axel-faes?). Venues, people, organisations, radar, literature, leads, pillars, domains, data, resources and statements are registries; applications, funding, education, events and tasks hold someone's work. A collection's schema already marks a field required and the schema check raises its absence (`findings.schema.required`, `data/snapshot/schema/check.ts:226`). (a) naming no one is a finding: a project manifest without `lead:` is a finding, as one without a known `tier:` is (ruling 206 (1)), and O55's hub rewrite gives the 10 their `lead:`; a collection declares the three fields in its own `schema:` and marks `lead` required when every entry has one, the existing schema check raising a missing one; O55's hub rewrite declares them, `lead` required, on `applications.yml`, `funding.yml`, `education.yml`, `events.yml` and `tasks.yml`, writing each entry's people once (funding's and education's `Role` meta rows rewritten into the fields); a collection that declares none of the three has no roles, so its view offers no Mine only toggle, Lead badge or Role facet; there is no role for naming no one. Pro: missing people are found, not guessed, and a registry is never filtered by role. Con: the hub rewrite touches 10 manifests and every entry of five collections. (b) a project or entry that names no one takes the first role of the list (lead), and every collection takes the toggle. Pro: no rewrite. Con: a grant led by someone else that names no one shows as the user's lead, and a missing lead is never found. With `self` empty (`config/options/sources.yml:118-124`: "Empty marks nobody"), every entry is `other` and Mine only, on by default, would hide every entry: under (a) and (b) alike, a workspace whose `self` names no one offers no Mine only toggle, Lead badge or Role facet, and the boards' `role:` key is not applied. Recommendation: (a). (b) is the lazy option. (3) The tier flags once `projects`, `coauthor` and `archive` become `research` (Axel 2026-10-24: special behaviour attached to a tier is wrong; the `idea` flag is dropped, agreed). Every reader of a tier flag today: `main`, read by the venue load (`venueLoad`, `state/boards/ticker.ts:502`, through `state/feeds/feeds.ts:90` and `ticker.ts:159`, which counts every project with a venue whatever its stage), quick add (`liveProjects`, `state/boards/focus.ts:449`) and the tier of a new project whose form names none (`server/edit/writes/manifest.ts:615`); `software`, read by the card's Software line (`softwareTags`, `state/boards/board/items.ts:41-50`, and its label, `views/shared/card/project/CardLinks.svelte:84`) and by the board's New project button, hidden on a software board (`views/boards/projects/Projects.svelte:130`); `personal`, read by `isPersonal` (`server/content/audit/rules.ts:309`), which skips seven audit rule groups for the 17 personal projects: markers, gaps (no venue, missing field, provisional), conformance (README, folder name, code README, stage and folder), guardrails, readability (`server/content/audit/projects.ts:146,205,309,376,421`) and the publication feed (`audit/publications.ts:178,233`); `finished`, read by the Map, the venue and pillar counts, the cross-link groups and the audit (`graph.ts:97`, `venues.ts:37`, `collection.ts:787`, `projects.ts:379`); `outcome_folders` and `stage_folders`, read by the folder check (`folderStage`, `projects.ts:296-304`). (a) `tiers:` carries no flags, each tier a word and its label: `finished` goes and its readers read the stage flag `terminal`; `idea`, `outcome_folders` and `stage_folders` go, and the folder check and `audit.msg_projects_published` come back in O63 as ruling 220's hub setting (between the two landings neither finding is raised); `main` goes: the venue load counts every project with a venue at a `live` stage, quick add offers every project not at a `terminal` stage, and a new project whose form names no tier gets the first tier of the list; `software` goes: the Software line joins the Related line, which reads `related:` both ways (a project's own and those naming it), and every board offers New project, writing the board's tier; `personal` goes: the audit's research rules skip the tiers a list `research_audit: { skip_tiers: [personal] }` in `config/catalogs/vocab.yml` names. Pro: a tier is only a word a board filters on; each rule names its own scope. Con: one new key for the audit's scope. (b) as (a), with `personal: true` kept as the one tier flag. Pro: no new key. Con: one behaviour still hangs on a tier. Recommendation: (a) (Axel 2026-10-24 agreed: the audit names its own scope, `tiers:` carries no flags). The lazy option is keeping the flags `main` and `software` as they are. Closed 2026-10-24: Axel ruled (1) (a), (2) (a), (3) (a) ("Agreed"); booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 287 (2026-10-23, Axel, at O67's landing): rename the Focus board's Backlog lane to Idea. Today the lane is `{ key: backlog, label: Backlog, cluster: backlog, stages: [nascent] }` in `config/catalogs/vocab.yml:184`, the tier flag `backlog` (`vocab.yml:208-214`) says which tiers' nascent projects sit on it, and the code names it as `BACKLOG` in `state/boards/focus.ts:43` and `views/boards/focus/lanes/Lanes.svelte`; HELP.md, data-map.md, `vocab.ts`, `Focus.svelte`, `derive.ts` and `server/edit/writes/manifest.ts` carry the word. The word idea already names the stage flag `idea` (nascent and scoping, the cross-link group of ideas and the unplaced row) and the cluster heading 'Incubation: idea to project' over Scope, De-risk and Build. (a) the label only: the lane draws 'Idea', and its key, its cluster and the tier flag stay `backlog`; pro, a one-line change; con, the config and the code call the lane by one name and the board by another. (b) the whole name: the lane key, the cluster and the tier flag become `idea`, the label 'Idea', with the constant, the documents, the comments and the tests; pro, one name everywhere; con, `idea` is then both the lane (nascent only) and the stage flag (nascent and scoping), so the flag's comment must say the lane holds only its first stage. (c) keep Backlog. Recommendation (b); (a) is the lazy option. The work belongs to no step yet: it is folded into O55 or made a step of its own only on Axel's word. Closed 2026-10-23: Axel ruled (b), folded into O55; booked in plan.md Rulings and O55's line.
- 286 (2026-10-23, O67's landing, ruling 271): the hub's data and the workspace repositories' manifests rewritten once for O67, shown as a diff and committed on Axel's word. The diff: `agents/reviews/O67-hub-rewrite.patch` (223 files of the hub: the 110 manifests, `README.md`, `.oculus/config.yml`, `data.yml`, `venues.yml`'s help line, the UGent letter's source ledger, `deck/present.yml`, and the four folder moves `archive/nogo/*` and `archive/disproved/*` to `archive/failed/`, `archive/dropped/*` to `archive/stopped/`, `coauthor/abandoned/*` to `coauthor/stopped/`) and `agents/reviews/O67-workspace-manifests.patch` (cathedra, game-engine, oculus, officium, ordo, scotoma, theaxec.github.io, youtube-downloader). (b) remove `ecg-af-fair` from the group's slides: pro, the group holds only pre-run ideas; con, ECG-AF (FAIR)'s slide leaves the deck. - 285 (2026-10-23, O39's look C8): a reload draws the default arrangement for about 0.2 s before the saved one. In the second checkout Ember run the page had a Funding tab open in front of Focus; after Cmd+R it drew the Focus tab from 64.55 s to 64.77 s into the recording, then the Funding tab again. The cause, read in `src/lib/state/workbench/layout/saved/storage.ts:5-9`: the saved arrangement is read only once the workspace answer has named the workspace, so the page's first draw is the default arrangement. No step in the plan changes the layout's load (O55, O60, O63 and O66 do not), so folding it into one under Z468 would put it in a step it does not belong to, and a new step needs your permission. Options: (a) a new step after O66: the page draws its panes only once the saved arrangement is read, or the server sends the arrangement with the first HTML as part E does with the theme, the choice taken in its brief; (b) folded into O55, which changes the Focus and Projects boards but not the layout's load; (c) left as it is. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: the flash is seen on every reload and at every start with a saved arrangement, and the first-HTML route already exists for the theme; (b) puts the fix in a step about something else; (c) is the lazy option. Closed 2026-10-23: Axel ruled (b); folded into O55's line, booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 284 (2026-10-23, O67's brief check, ruling 283 (4) and (2)): three cases of a move on the Focus board that ruling 283 does not settle the way it intends (`agents/reviews/O67-brief-check.md`, findings 6.5, 6.4 and 8). (1) A card on Backburner or Blocked whose stage the target lane already holds, such as a `submit-ready` card carrying `blocked` dropped on Push. Ruling 283 (4) says a drop on a stage lane writes that lane's first stage, so the card becomes `in-prep` and loses its submit-ready stage. Options: (a) the stage is kept when the target lane holds it, and `parked` and `blocked` leave the chips, so the card moves to Push still `submit-ready`; (b) as ruled, the card is written `in-prep`. Recommendation: (a), since a move is meant to change the lane, and (b) writes a stage the project is not at. (2) A card carrying both `parked` and `blocked` sits on Blocked; dropped on Backburner, ruling 283 (4) adds `parked`, which it already has, so nothing changes and the card stays on Blocked. Options: (a) a drop on Backburner or Blocked adds that lane's chip and removes the other lane's chip, so the card moves where it was dropped; (b) as ruled, the drop does nothing. Recommendation: (a), which is the reason ruling 283 (4) gave for removing the chips on a stage lane. (3) The backlog lane's line under its heading, which ruling 283 (2) (a) keeps as today's text, "idea backlog: drag onto a lane to start, or drop here to park". "Park" now names the chip `parked` and the Backburner lane, while a drop on the backlog makes the card `nascent`. Options: (a) "ideas not started yet: drag onto a lane to start, or drop here to make it an idea again"; (b) keep today's line. Recommendation: (a). The brief check's other findings are closed in the brief by changes that stay inside the rulings, among them: the three code readers of the removed free-text `venue` (`items.ts:86`, `project-context.ts:10`, `palette.ts:177`) added to the step; the backlog kept in title order and out of the Awaiting box, as now; a move computed by one state function so its cases can be run; the move refused when the lane's stage or chip is not a word of `stages:` or `chips:`, so a write is never refused in part; a lane row with both `stages` and `chips`, or neither, and a stage held by two rows refused by the vocabulary's shape; the remaining stale lines in the catalogs, documents and comments named. None of these leaves work undone. Nothing of O67 is built until you rule. Closed 2026-10-23: Axel ruled (1) (a), (2) (a), (3) (a); booked in plan.md Rulings, carried by O67.

- 263 (2026-10-23, O39 part F's review): two rule texts disagree on whether a builder may read git. spec.md "Where you work" says a builder runs no git command, read-only ones included, and every repair-round brief repeats it. The `plan-orchestration` skill's text for the builder's dispatch message says that reading with `git status`, `git diff` and `git show` is fine, and part F's builder ran such reads on that word. Skills are never edited, so the choice is in spec.md or in the dispatch messages. Options: (a) spec.md allows a builder the three read-only forms the skill names (`git status`, `git diff`, `git show`), and nothing else; (b) spec.md stands, and every dispatch message from now on says that no git command is run, read-only ones included, against the skill's text. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: the three reads change nothing, the reviewer already runs the same three, and a builder that compares its tree with the base copy by hand does the same work slower and with more room for a wrong read; (b) keeps a ban that only costs the builder time. Until you rule, part F's git reads are not counted as a finding. Closed 2026-10-23: Axel ruled (a); `agents/spec.md` "Where you work" and the hub-data paragraph now allow the three reads; booked in plan.md Rulings.
- 262 (2026-10-23, the reports of O61 and O53): both builders ran their cases through programs their briefs did not build, and each brief says no script is written for the step without your approval quoted from a line of `plan.md`'s Rulings. O61 wrote a probe test file, `tests/o61-probe.test.ts`, into a copy of the base tree, ran it there (18 failed, 3 passed of 21) and in its worktree after the change (21 passed), then deleted it; nothing of it is in the diff. O53 ran its cases as `node --input-type=module -` programs given on the command line, with no file written (its report, judgment J1). Until you rule, the case results those programs gave are not used, and neither step lands. Options: (a) approve, for these two steps and the steps after them, a case-run program that only prints the result of each case of the brief, writes nothing into the step's diff, and has its source and output quoted in the report; the results of O61 and O53 are then used, and each brief from now on states this approval; (b) do not approve: the results are discarded, each case is judged by the reviewer and at landing by reading the code and by the tests the step has, and each brief from now on forbids such programs outright; (c) do not approve for the future, but accept these two runs once. Recommendation: (a), a judgment: a case run computes one fact per input (what the code gives for it), which is what the hub's rule lets a script do, and a quoted run is better evidence than a reading; (b) is the lazy option, since it throws away runs already made and replaces evidence with a reading; (c) leaves the next builder in the same place. Closed 2026-10-23: Axel ruled (a); `agents/spec.md` "What every package obeys" states the case-run program rule; the results of O61's probe and O53's programs are used; booked in plan.md Rulings.

- 259 (2026-10-22, O65): Pin Editor and Unpin Editor show the chord Cmd+K Shift+Enter in the palette, but no key runs it, on main or in O65; with Cmd+K now the editor's chord key (ruling 258 (b)), the hint names a key that does nothing. Options: (a) bind Cmd+K Shift+Enter in the editor to pin and unpin the tab, as VS Code does, in O65's repair round; (b) keep the hint as it is, naming a key that does nothing; (c) drop the hint, the lazy option. Recommendation: (a), since the palette then tells the truth and the key behaves as in VS Code. - 258 (2026-10-22, O65's brief check): the step line of O65 (ruling 250) says the editor's keys work as VS Code's do and every default key Monaco binds that fails is fixed at its cause; D46 (ruling 28) says Cmd+K stays as it is, the palette's. The two collide on the keys the editor gives up to the palette (`src/lib/ui/controls/editor/chords.ts`, `freedKeys`): Cmd+K and its 31 chords (in VS Code, Cmd+K Cmd+C comments a line and Cmd+K Cmd+0 folds all, inside the editor; in oculus they run only from the palette), and on macOS Ctrl+K (in VS Code, Delete All Right in the editor; in oculus, the palette). F1, F8 and Shift+F8 are not in the clash: VS Code's workbench takes them too.
  - (a) Cmd+K and Ctrl+K stay the palette's everywhere, as D46 says; O65's key table lists the Cmd+K chords and Ctrl+K as given up to the palette under D46, each still run from the palette. Pro: nothing you press today changes. Con: the editor keeps differing from VS Code on 32 keys, which is what O65's line asks to end. This is the lazy option: it records the difference instead of ending it.
  - (b) Recommended. Inside the editor, Cmd+K is VS Code's chord key: Cmd+K then the second key runs Monaco's chord, and on macOS Ctrl+K is Delete All Right; outside the editor Cmd+K stays the palette, and in the editor the palette opens with Cmd+Shift+P, Cmd+P and F1, as in VS Code. Pro: the editor's keys are VS Code's, which O65's line asks for. Con: Cmd+K pressed in the editor no longer opens the palette; D46 changes for the editor.
  - (c) Inside the editor, Cmd+K waits for a second key: a key of one of Monaco's chords runs that chord, and any other key, or no key within the chord time, opens the palette; Ctrl+K on macOS is Delete All Right. Pro: both uses of Cmd+K stay. Con: the palette opens only after the wait or the second key, a delay on every Cmd+K in the editor, and VS Code has no such rule.
  Ruled 2026-10-22 by Axel: (b), booked in plan.md Rulings; O65's step line carries it (ruling 258).
- Open item 250 (2026-10-22, Axel's request; editor keys in general, Cmd+/ one example; fails in a Chrome tab; not locked): two parts. (1) The status bar scrolls left and right when its items do not fit. (2) The editor's keys work as VS Code's do. Run on TEST in Chrome on the fixture `tests/fixtures/hubs/demo/projects/active/streaming-anomaly/manuscript/main.tex` (each change undone, `git status` clean): Cmd+/ does nothing; typing `{` adds no `}`; Cmd+L selects nothing; Cmd+] and Alt+Down work. In `vocab.yml` Cmd+/ and Cmd+F work. Cause, read in the code: Monaco toggles a comment and closes a bracket from a language's settings (`setLanguageConfiguration`), and the editor registers its Shiki languages with an id only (`src/lib/ui/controls/editor/kit/load.ts:355-357`); `kit/rite/settings.ts` gives settings to rite, CMake, both Fortran forms, Jinja and Svelte alone, and Monaco has none of its own for `latex`, `bibtex`, `shellscript` (bash and `.slurm`), `make`, `matlab`, `ocaml`, `common-lisp` and `diff`, so in those eight Cmd+/, bracket closing, surrounding a selection and comment-aware indentation do nothing (the seven besides `latex` by reading, not run). Cmd+L is taken in a LaTeX file as the first key of the LaTeX tab's chords (`src/lib/data/helpers/keys/oculus/editor-keys.ts`, `LATEX_CHORD_FIRST`), so Monaco's select-line never runs there; Cmd+Alt+[ and ] are taken by promote and demote. The step: the eight languages get their comment, bracket, auto-closing and surrounding settings in `kit/rite/settings.ts`, with a table test that every language the editor registers has a comment rule; every default key Monaco binds is pressed in a `.tex`, a `.slurm` and a `.yml` file in Chrome and each one that fails is fixed at its cause; the LaTeX keys that take a VS Code editor key are set against what VS Code with LaTeX Workshop binds (not verified yet). (a) One new step O65 holding the status bar and the editor keys. (b) The editor keys as their own step O66, the status bar as O65. Recommended: (b), one concern each; the key part now has a found cause and a check over every key. Neither leaves work undone. Needs Axel's explicit permission for a new step. Ruled 2026-10-22 by Axel: (a), booked in plan.md Rulings; O65 added to the step list.
- Open item 251 (2026-10-22, Axel's question, redone on his reply, O53): which field names a project's people. Measured over the 113 manifests under `projects/` and `side-projects/` (a Python read of each `project.yml`): `authors:` is free text in 62 (7 of them one string, not a list), the longest list 8 names (`go-bttr`), 63 distinct strings in all, 37 of them in one manifest only; one person is spelled several ways (`Faes`, `Axel Faes`, `... Faes`; `van den Berg`, `Stephanie M. van den Berg`, `Stephanie van den Berg`), and 3 lists are cut with `...` or `et al.`; about 38 of the names are people the registry (19 people) does not hold, spread over the coauthors of the hub's papers, no single list. `collaborators:` holds person keys in 36 manifests. Oculus reads `authors:` only to draw the authors line of a project card and the Authors column of a board's list mode (`board/presets.ts:369-380`, `ProjectCard.svelte:384`, `config/defaults/layout.yml` columns); the full byline of each published paper is in the website's `_data/publications.yml` and in the manuscript. (a) A project names its people by person keys only: `lead:` (O53, a list of keys) and `collaborators:` (the registry people who work on it, the name kept); `authors:` leaves the manifest schema; the card's authors line and the Authors column draw the names of `lead:` and `collaborators:` from the registry, the lead first; the byline stays in the manuscript and on the website; the registry grows only by the people a manifest names as a key. Pro: one home per person, no free text, no long tail of one-paper coauthors in the registry. Con: a card no longer shows a paper's full byline in its order. (b) `authors:` becomes the one field, an ordered list of person keys, and `collaborators:` goes; every byline name becomes a registry person, about 38 new people. Pro: the byline order kept on the card. Con: the registry fills with coauthors the hub names nowhere else, which Axel rejected. (c) Both fields as they are: the lazy option, two places for one fact and free-text names. Recommended: (a). How the hub's 62 `authors:` values are removed is open item 254. Axel likes (a), not locked, and asked how users choose which people to add when not every author lands in `collaborators:`. Answer: under (a) nobody copies an author list; the user picks the people they work with. The Collaborators field is a picker over the people list (`config/schemas/manifest-schema.yml:57`, `type: chips, from: people`): it offers the people already in the list and cannot take a typed new name (`src/lib/views/shared/forms/entry/edit/FieldControl.svelte:135-146`, free text only for `type: tags`). A person not yet in the list is added on the People board with Add, which asks the name and opens the new person's page (`config/defaults/sources.yml:28`), then picked. A variant of (a) for Axel: (a2) as (a), and the picker also offers "Add person <typed name>" when the typed name matches nobody, which writes the new person to the people list and picks them in one step; pro: no detour to the People board; con: a new control shape on every person picker. The paper's full author list, in its printed order, stays in the manuscript and on the website. Recommended after Axel's question: (a2), since naming a new coauthor while editing a project is the common case and the detour to the People board costs every such edit; the new control is one entry at the foot of the picker's list. (c), both fields kept, is the lazy option. Ruled 2026-10-22 by Axel: (a2), booked in plan.md Rulings and on O53's line.

The closest word is the one at the smallest edit distance (Levenshtein), ties to the first in the list's order, over at most about 60 words; oculus has no such function (`grep -rniE "closest|levenshtein|editDistance" src`: none). (a) `leven` 4.1.0 (MIT, Sindre Sorhus, last release 2025-09-11, ESM, no dependencies) added as a dependency: pro, a maintained one-function package; con, one more dependency for a few lines. (b) `fastest-levenshtein` 1.0.16 (MIT, ka-weihe, last release 2022-08-02, no dependencies): pro, the fastest; con, no release in three years, and speed does not matter over 60 words. (c) written by hand in `src/lib/data/shapes/vocab-keys.ts` beside `wordFlag`: the two-row edit distance, about 15 lines, with a table test: pro, no dependency; con, a function oculus keeps. Recommended: (c), since the input is a few dozen short words and the function is small and fully tested by its table; none of the three leaves work undone, so none is the lazy option. Ruled 2026-10-22 by Axel: (c), booked in plan.md Rulings and on O61's line.

- Open item 240 (2026-10-22): how the landing's check follows the standing demand "Only related tests at a landing". The `land` skill's `land.sh` runs the plan's verify list, `npm run test:quick`, on main; that list runs the essential list `tests/tiers/essential.txt`, tests related to no changed file, and leaves out the related tests under `tests/checks` and the ones tagged slow (`tests/tiers/quick.mjs:1-28`, `:203-205`, `:272`).
  - (a) `tests/tiers/quick.mjs` gains a related-only selection (no essential list; the related and changed tests under `tests/checks` and tagged slow included; no 45 s limit), and it becomes the verify list for builders, reviewers and the landing alike. Pro: one command, `land.sh` follows the demand unchanged, builders' runs get shorter too. Con: builders and reviewers lose the essential list as a wide net.
  - (b) The same selection, used at the landing only, `verify:` kept as it is for builders and reviewers. Pro: builders keep the essential net. Con: `land.sh` always runs `verify:`, so the landing replays the script's steps by hand with the other command, departing from the skill's script at every landing.
  - Ruled (a) by Axel, 2026-10-22. The lazy option was to keep `npm run test:quick` at the landing as it is, which breaks the demand and is not offered. The change to `quick.mjs` lands at the first landing after the ruling.
- Open item 241 (2026-10-22): where the landing's tests run. Standing demand Z492 says the landing gate never runs in main's checkout but in `.agents/worktrees/gate`; the `land` skill's `land.sh` runs its check on main, and the landings of O58, O59 and O62 ran on main (plan.md, their bookings: "Verification on main").
  - (a) On main, as `land.sh` does; Z492 retired. Pro: the skill's own script, and related tests take seconds. Con: main's checkout is the one Axel's server on 8790 runs from, and the test run's `svelte-kit sync` rewrites its `.svelte-kit` types.
  - (b) In the gate worktree, as Z492 says, the staged change then copied to main. Pro: Axel's running server never sees a test run. Con: every landing applies the range twice and keeps the gate's `node_modules` current, outside `land.sh`.
  - Ruled (b) by Axel, 2026-10-22, since it ends the cause Z492 was written for. (a) is the lazy option.

  - (b) G35's text widened with the same clause, its chat row unchanged as the test. Pro: no new entry. Con: G35 then guards two unrelated guarantees, which the one-guarantee-per-entry form of `guarded.md` does not take.
